Rebecca Maria Kuntz
1

Abstract




The Latent Information Geometry of Jet Classification

1
Zentrum für Astronomie der Universität Heidelberg,

Astronomisches Rechen-Institut, Germany

2
Institut für Theoretische Physik, Universität Heidelberg, Germany

3
Interdisciplinary Center for Scientific Computing (IWR), Universität Heidelberg, Germany

August 10, 2026

Abstract

Latent representations are an important theme in modern machine learning. Any network training with the notion of locality introduces a latent geometry which we can analyze with the help of differential geometry, specifically information geometry. We introduce the main concepts needed to analyze learned latent geometries, specifically curvature and nonmetricities, and show how they can be used for decoder and classifier geometries. We then apply our new methods to understand the physics behind binary quark-gluon classification and three-fold fat jet tagging.

Contents

1
Introduction

A leading theme of modern machine learning (ML) for fundamental physics
[
68
]
is representation learning. Starting with exploiting structures or symmetries to increase performance, it leads us directly to explainability, which requires a mathematically sound and reliable framework in fundamental physics. Quantitatively understanding a complex network output or classifier decision allows us to learn about the underlying physics and strengthens confidence in the soundness of trained neural networks. If we manage to relate latent and feature spaces we can answer questions like: What physical features does the network rely on? Does the latent representation align with given physics features? How does such an alignment change with the network task?

We propose a new way to analyze latent representations based on the simple assumption that networks encode a task-specific correlations or similarity in a local structure. A variational autoencoder
[
46
]
realizes this through an explicit regularization, but self-attention
[
83
]
in transformers can be viewed in complete analogy. Given the local structure, we can study the geometry of the latent representation using differential geometry. Because network training can be viewed as statistical inference of a meaningful latent representation, we first turn concepts of General Relativity and its alternative formulations into the analogous information geometry. Starting with the Fisher information as a metric, we can study the latent geometry, curvature, nonmetricity, and geodesic distances, and in turn relate the learned representations to the physical feature space.

An interesting and relevant LHC task for this kind of study is modern jet tagging
[
25
]
, where quark-gluon tagging
[
66
,
34
,
48
,
22
,
49
,
42
,
53
,
57
,
58
,
43
,
74
,
27
,
17
,
12
,
8
,
76
,
26
]
is in many ways the most challenging application and affects a vast number of LHC analyses, typically separating quark jets produced in weak decays from QCD background jets. The theoretical background of quark-gluon is challenging, because quark and gluon jets are ill-defined beyond leading order in QCD
[
35
]
. Furthermore, it strongly depends on the parton shower, hadronization, and detector effects
[
35
,
18
]
. If we want to trust quark-gluon tagging in LHC analyses, we need to understand what it is based on. Once we do understand some of its intricacies, we can apply this insight to close the simulation gap from simulation-based training and improve the resilience of top taggers
[
44
,
61
,
75
,
56
,
85
]
,
W/Z
taggers
[
21
,
24
,
45
,
59
]
, and bottom/charm taggers
[
82
,
36
,
9
,
10
]
.

Modern machine learning enables quark-gluon tagging through graph-based classification networks like the classic ParticleNet
[
71
]
. It represents modern taggers operating on a point cloud of low-level detector inputs or jet constituents. Advanced graph-based architectures, including advanced transformer implementations
[
73
,
37
,
86
,
14
,
78
,
30
,
67
]
, have led to significant advances. To understand these graph-based networks we employ our information geometry methodology. While it is clear that low-dimensional Euclidean latent representations are not sufficient to encode all tagging-relevant information, the question is how this information is encoded. Curvature, nonmetricity and torsion offer three possibilities at least in principle. Going beyond binary classification, the separation of jets in terms of latent distance in the learned geometry becomes a key question.

In Sec.
2
we describe our information geometry methodology. In Sec.
3
we apply it to a toy classification problem between digits, where we control the relevant geometric features. We show how four new scalars for information geometry capture much of the relevant information about the learned decoder and classifier geometries. In Sec.
4
we then apply these concepts to quark-gluon tagging and relate the learned geometries to the known jet shapes. Finally, we extend our framework to 3-label classification of top vs
Z
vs
q/g
jets in Sec.
5
and link the latent space geometries to simple observables like the N-subjettiness and the jet mass. We provide an outlook and a detailed development of the theory in a series of appendices.

2
Information geometry methodology

Information geometry applies differential geometry to decision making
[
5
,
65
]
. It is based on the fact that a parametric family of probability distributions,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=\{p_{\theta}:\,\theta\in\Theta\}\qquad\quad p:\Theta\rightarrow\mathcal{P(\mathcal{X})}\;,

(1)

constitutes a topological space and thereby a notion of closeness. Here,
\Theta\subseteq\mdmathbb{R}^{n}
is the parameter space and
\mathcal{X}
the (sample) data space
[
65
]
. This topological space
\mathcal{P}
becomes a statistical manifold
\mathcal{M}
, where the model parameters
\theta
constitute a local coordinate chart,
i.e.
a map to Euclidean space. A reparametrization of the statistical model then corresponds to a coordinate change on the manifold, and geometric invariants correspond to the key properties of the statistical model which are invariant under reparametrization
[
65
]
. In our study, such a statistical model will be a classifier or a decoder network.

Metric space

A metric tensor is a symmetric positive-definite bilinear form which allows us to measure infinitesimal distances on the manifold. If we identify it with the Fisher information
[
31
]
,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{g^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}{g^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}{g^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}{g^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}(\theta)\equiv\mathchoice{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}(\theta)=\int\text{d}\mu(x)\,p_{\theta}(x)\partial_{i}\log p_{\theta}(x)\,\partial_{j}\log p_{\theta}(x)\qquad\text{with}\qquad\partial_{i}\equiv\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{i}}\;,

(2)

the statistical manifold becomes Riemannian with a measure
\text{d}\mu
over the data space. The Fisher information is the unique Riemannian metric invariant under sufficient statistics
[
6
,
65
]
. The relation of the Fisher information metric to the quality of a potential optimal measurement is illustrated by the Cramér-Rao bound. For an unbiased measurement, the uncertainty of the measurement is given by the covariance matrix at
\theta
. The Cramér-Rao bound states that this covariance matrix is always larger than the inverse Fisher information. This bound can be used to compute the optimal outcome of multi-dimensional LHC precision measurements
[
15
,
16
]
.

We can construct a differential structure on the statistical manifold. It globally connects different tangent spaces
T_{p}\mathcal{M}
, where the connection allows us to differentiate one vector field
X\in\Gamma(T\mathcal{M})
with respect to another
Y\in\Gamma(T\mathcal{M})
as
\nabla_{Y}X
. In such an induced coordinate frame, the
k
-th component of the covariant derivative is related to the connection coefficients
\Gamma
as

\displaystyle(\nabla_{Y}X)^{k}=Y^{i}\left(\frac{\partial X^{k}}{\partial Y^{i}}+\mathchoice{\Gamma^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{j}}}}{\Gamma^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{j}}}}{\Gamma^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{j}}}}{\Gamma^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\mak (3)

The unique connection of Riemannian geometry, the Levi-Civita-connection (LC)
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-14.75882pt{\text{LC}}\kern 8.04631pt}}_{{\kern-29.28188pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.04631pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-14.75882pt{\text{LC}}\kern 8.04631pt}}_{{\kern-29.28188pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.04631pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-10.27547pt{\text{LC}}\kern 5.44908pt}}_{{\kern-20.64906pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.44908pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-9.1088pt{\text{LC}}\kern 4.28241pt}}_{{\kern-19.48239pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.28241pt}}}
, is characterized by two properties: it is torsion-free and metric compatible. Therefore, the LC-connection is free to express invariant information as scalar curvature. However, the LC-connection alone is unable to fully capture the geometry of decision making. We need a different way to construct such a geometry, which in general has LC-curvature, nonmetricity, but no torsion
[
64
,
65
]
.

Divergence-induced geometry

D:\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mdmathbb{R}_{0+}
allow us to construct an information geometry
[
29
]
,

metric tensor

The asymmetry
D[p_{\theta},p_{\theta^{\prime}}]\neq D[p_{\theta^{\prime}},p_{\theta}]
expresses the innate duality of information geometry. It is manifest in the existence of two connections
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{D}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.1929pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.99492pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-12.35033pt{D}\kern (5)

Amari
\alpha
-geometry and Amari-Chentsov tensor

One particular choice of divergence is the
\alpha
-divergence
[
2
]
,

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}[p_{\theta},p_{\theta^{\prime}}]=\frac{4}{1-\alpha^{2}}\left(1-\int\text{d}x\,p_{\theta}(x)^{(1-\alpha)/2}p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)^{(1+\alpha)/2}\right)\;.

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{k}\mathchoice{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{ij}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{ij}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{ij}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{ij}}}}+\partial_{j}\mathchoice{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.39331pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13808pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{ik}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.39331pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13808pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{ik}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.39331pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13808pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{ik}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.39331pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13808pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{ik}}}}-\partial_{i}\mathchoice{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.00354pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.57394pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{jk}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.00354pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.57394pt][c]{$\ \displaystyle C_{ijk}

\displaystyle=\int\text{d}\mu(x)\,p_{\theta}\,\partial_{i}\log p_{\theta}\,\partial_{j}\log p_{\theta}\,\partial_{k}\log p_{\theta}\;.

(7)

Here,
C_{ijk}
is the symmetric rank-(0,3) Amari-Chentsov tensor (ACT) or skewness tensor. In analogy to the Fisher information, the ACT is the unique rank-3 tensor invariant under sufficient statistics
[
64
,
65
]
.

KL-divergence geometry

In the limit
\alpha\rightarrow\pm 1
, one can show that the
\alpha
-divergence becomes the forward (inverse) KL-divergence
[
4
]

\displaystyle D_{\text{KL}}[p_{\theta},p_{\theta^{\prime}}]=\int\text{d}\mu(x)\;p_{\theta}(x)\,\log\frac{p_{\theta}(x)}{p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)}\;,

(8)

for which we can confirm that the Fisher metric in Eq.(
2
) follows from Eq.(
4
)

\displaystyle-\partial_{i}\partial_{j^{\prime}}D_{\text{KL}}[p_{\theta},p_{\theta^{\prime}}]\Big|_{\theta=\theta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}\int\text{d}\mu(x)\;p_{\theta}(x)\;\partial_{j^{\prime}}\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)\Big|_{\theta=\theta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\int\text{d}\mu(x)\;p_{\theta}(x)\;\partial_{i}\log p_{\theta}(x)\,\partial_{j}\log p_{\theta}(x)=F_{ij}(\theta)\;.

(9)

Accordingly, the connection coefficients in Eq.(
7
) become

We can compute the ACT from the
(\pm 1)
-connection coefficients. From a statistical point of view, it quantifies the skewness of the likelihood and arises as

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{(+1)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-15.43332pt{(+1)}\kern 5.25pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-39.76991pt\mathchoice{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.25pt{ijk}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(+1)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-10.98056pt{(+1)}\kern 3.67499pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-28.36388pt\mathchoice{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.67499pt{ijk}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(+1)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-9.93057pt{(+1)}\kern 2.625pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-27.31389pt\mathchoice{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.625pt{ijk}}}}=-\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\partial_{k^{\prime}}D_{\text{KL}}[p_{\theta},p_{\theta^{\prime}}]+\partial_{k}\partial_{i^{\prime}}\partial_{j^{\prime}}D_{\text{KL}}[p_{\theta},p_{\theta^{\prime}}]\Bigg|_{\theta=\theta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\int\text{d}\mu(x)\;p_{\theta}(x)\;\partial_{j}\log p_{\theta}(x)\partial_{k^{\prime}}\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)-\partial_{k}\int\text{d}\mu(x)\;p_{\theta}(x)\;\partial_{i^{\prime}}\partial_{j^{\prime}}\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)\Bigg|_{\theta=\theta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\text{d}\mu(x)\;p_{\theta}(x)\;\partial_{i}\log p_{\theta}(x)\partial_{j}\log p_{\theta}(x)\partial_{k^{\prime}}\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)

\displaystyle+\int\text{d}\mu(x)\;p_{\theta}(x)\;\partial_{k^{\prime}}\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)\;\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\log p_{\theta}(x)

\displaystyle-\int\text{d}\mu(x)\;p_{\theta}(x)\;\partial_{k}\log p_{\theta}(x)\;\partial_{i^{\prime}}\partial_{j^{\prime}}\log p_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)\Big)\Bigg|_{\theta=\theta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\text{d}\mu(x)\;p_{\theta}(x)\;\partial_{i}\log p_{\theta}(x)\partial_{j}\log p_{\theta}(x)\partial_{k}\log p_{\theta}(x)\;,

(11)

which gives the ACT in Eq.(
7
).

Riemann curvature and Ricci scalar

Figure 1
:
Left: Riemann curvature changes the orientation of a vector when it is parallel-transported in a loop. Right: the Amari-Chentsov tensor causes length and angular defects of vectors under parallel transport.

The left side of Fig.
1
illustrates the effects of positive scalar curvature on the example of the 2-sphere: when a vector is parallel-transported in a closed loop on this manifold, a mismatch between the orientations of the initial and the final vectors emerges. This angular defect of the geometry is quantified by the Ricci curvature scalar
R
. As an essential property of the manifold it follows directly from the Riemann curvature tensor

Curvature, nonmetricity, and the ACT

There are three main ways to encode information in a connection: curvature, nonmetricity, and torsion. In standard General Relativity, the LC-connection
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-14.75882pt{\text{LC}}\kern 8.04631pt}}_{{\kern-29.28188pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.04631pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-14.75882pt{\text{LC}}\kern 8.04631pt}}_{{\kern-29.28188pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.04631pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-10.27547pt{\text{LC}}\kern 5.44908pt}}_{{\kern-20.64906pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.44908pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-9.1088pt{\text{LC}}\kern 4.28241pt}}_{{\kern-19.48239pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.28241pt}}}
is assumed to map out curvature, encoded in the Riemann tensor, and to covariantly preserve the metric,
\nabla_{\text{LC}}\,g=0
. Nonmetricity is an important concept in Weyl geometry formulations of gravity, which instead treat the geometry as flat and give up the assumption of metricity,
\nabla g\neq 0
. In this case, a nonmetricity tensor
Q_{ijk}\equiv\nabla_{i}g_{jk}
quantifies geometry
[
60
,
39
]
.

In information geometry, nonmetricity appears because the
(\pm 1)
-connections of the KL-divergence do not separately preserve the metric tensor, as we can see in Eq.(
5
). The nonmetricity tensor for the
(\pm 1)
-connection is the ACT
[
20
,
4
]
,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\pm 1)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[18.30716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.30716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-18.22963pt{(\pm 1)}\kern 8.04631pt\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-42.56622pt\mathchoice{\makebox[18.30716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.30716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.04631pt{i}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\pm 1)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[18.30716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.30716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-18.22963pt{(\pm 1)}\kern 8.04631pt\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-42.56622pt\mathchoice{\makebox[18.30716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.30716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.04631pt{i}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\pm 1)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[18.30716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.30716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-12.75465pt{(\pm 1)}\kern 5.44908pt\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-30.13797pt\mathchoice{\makebox[18.30716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.30716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.44908pt{i}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\pm 1)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[18.30716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.30716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-11.58798pt{(\pm 1)}\kern 4.28241pt\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-28.9713pt\mathchoice{\makebox[18.30716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.30716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.28241pt{i}}}}F_{jk}=\pm C_{ijk}\;.

(14)

The right panel of Fig.
1
illustrates the geometric effect of nonmetricity: a non-vanishing ACT changes a vector’s length and direction under parallel-transport along a path on the statistical manifold. This leads to an an additional stretch, compression, or a shear of statistical distances. It is helpful to split the ACT into a trace and a part which is traceless in the last two indices,

\displaystyle C_{ijk}=\frac{1}{2}\tau_{i}\,F_{jk}+\tilde{C}_{ijk}\qquad\text{with}\qquad\text{tr}_{F}\tilde{C}=0\;.

(15)

The trace
\tau_{i}
is the Chebyshev (co-)vector field
[
62
]
. It is the information geometry analogue of the Weyl field in gravity theories and as such dictates the failure to preserve lengths on the statistical manifold
[
60
]
.

Figure 2
:
Illustration of parallel transport with the
(\pm 1)
- and LC-connection, respectively. Adapted from Ref.
[
65
]
.

Geodesics, autoparallels, and latent distance

To globally relate different points in the latent space and interpolate between them, we need to generalize the concept of straight lines and distances in flat, Euclidean space to a curved manifold. Given a connection, autoparallels are curves
\gamma:I\subset\mdmathbb{R}\to\mathcal{M}
, whose tangent vector
\dot{\gamma}\in\Gamma(T\mathcal{M})
remains parallel to itself when transported along the curve. In local coordinates, autoparallel curves fulfill the ODE

\displaystyle\ddot{\gamma}^{k}(t)+\mathchoice{\Gamma^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{j}}}}{\Gamma^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{j}}}}{\Gamma^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{j}}}}{\Gamma^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{j}}}}\dot{\gamma}^{i}(t)\dot{\gamma}^{j}(t)=0\;.

(16)

If the connection coefficients in this ODE are taken from the LC-connection, the curves
\gamma
are also length-minimizing geodesics,
i.e.
the straightest and shortest paths.
For the
(\pm 1)
-connections the ODE instead becomes

\displaystyle\ddot{\gamma}^{k}(t)+\left(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-11.96251pt{\text{LC}}\kern 5.25pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{j}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-11.96251pt{\text{LC}}\kern where the ACT pushes the dual autoparallels (

65
]
. This deviation of the dual autoparallels from the length-minimizing paths is illustrated in Fig.
2
.

As a measure of dissimilarity between two latent points, we therefore define the statistical Fisher-Rao distance along a path
\gamma
on the manifold, for instance, a geodesic or autoparallel
[
65
]
,

\displaystyle\rho_{F}(z_{i},z_{f})=\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}t\;\sqrt{F_{ab}(\gamma(t))\;\dot{\gamma}^{a}(t)\dot{\gamma}^{b}(t)}\qquad\text{for}\qquad\gamma:

\displaystyle t\in[0,1]\to z\in\mdmathbb{R}^{2}

\displaystyle z_{i}

\displaystyle=\gamma(0),\;z_{f}=\gamma(1)\;.

(18)

3
Decoder-classifier geometries and nonmetricity scalars

Figure 3
:
Conceptual overview of our network setup. The decoder and classifier are both attached to the latent space
z
and each induce a geometry on it.

As an illustration, we apply information geometry to the latent space of a variational autoencoder with an additional classifier head, shown in Fig.
3
. As the full data information is too complex for the latent space
z\in\mdmathbb{R}^{2}
, the network has to construct latent differential geometries, using curvature and nonmetricity, to encode information relevant for the decoding for the classification: Effectively, this corresponds to a highly nonlinear embedding of the data into the latent manifold. After introducing the classifier and decoder geometries, we go through the geometric quantities for analyzing the latent space alongside their statistical significance and illustrate them for subsets of the MNIST dataset. Crucially, we propose new scalars for information geometry and discuss their meaning.

Classifier Geometry

A multi-class probability in terms of the network parameters
\phi
is given by the softmax function

\displaystyle p_{\phi}(k|z)=\frac{e^{\eta_{\phi,k}(z)}}{\sum_{l}e^{\eta_{\phi,l}(z)}}\qquad\qquad k,l=1\penalty\ ...\penalty\ K\;.

(19)

For the discrete probability distribution, we keep the expectation values in the Fisher metric and the
\alpha
-connection

\displaystyle\mathchoice{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}(z)

\displaystyle=\Bigl\langle s_{i}(r)\,s_{j}(r)\Bigr\rangle_{r\sim p_{\phi}(r|z)}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{with}\quad s_{i}(r)

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}\log p_{\phi}(r|z)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\Gamma^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}{k}}}}{\Gamma^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}{k}}}}{\Gamma^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}{k}}}}{\Gamma^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}{k}}}}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{F^{{{il}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.39874pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.39874pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.24658pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.31898pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{{il}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.39874pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.39874pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.24658pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.31898pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{{il}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.39874pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.39874pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.24658pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.31898pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{{il}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.39874pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.39874pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.24658pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.31898pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\biggl\langle s_{l}(r)t_{jk}(r)+\frac{1-\alpha}{2}s_{l}(r)s_{j}(r)s_{k}(r)\biggr\rangle_{r\sim p_{\phi}(r|z)}

(20)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{F^{{{il}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.39874pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.39874pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.24658pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.31898pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{{il}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.39874pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.39874pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.24658pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.31898pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{{il}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.39874pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.39874pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.24658pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.31898pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{{il}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.39874pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.39874pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.24658pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.31898pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\left(\Bigl\langle s_{l}(r)t_{jk}(r)\Bigr\rangle_{r\sim p_{\phi}(r|z)}+\frac{1-\alpha}{2}C_{ljk}\right)\qquad

\displaystyle\text{with}\quad t_{jk}(r)

\displaystyle=\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\log p_{\phi}(r|z)\;.

Here, the nonmetricity or ACT can be finite.

Decoder geometry

The decoder is parametrized by a Gaussian with learned mean,

\displaystyle p_{\phi}(x^{\prime}|z)=\mathcal{N}(\mu_{\phi}(z),\sigma_{\phi})\;.

(21)

The variance does not depend on
z
. This Gaussian decoder yields simple analytic expressions for the Fisher metric, the connection coefficients, and the ACT,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{ij}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{ij}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{ij}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{ij}}}}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma_{\phi}^{2}}\,\partial_{i}\mu_{\phi}(z)\,\partial_{j}\mu_{\phi}(z)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-11.96251pt{\text{LC}}\kern 5.25pt{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-26.48557pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.25pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{j}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-11.96251pt{\text{LC}}\kern 5.25pt{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-26.48557pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.25pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{j}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-8.50137pt{\text{LC}}\kern 3.67499pt{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-18.87497pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.67499pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{j}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-7.45139pt{\text{LC}}\kern 2.625pt{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-17.82498pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.625pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}{j}}}}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma_{\phi}^{2}}\,\mathchoice{F^{{{k}{r}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.92825pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.92825pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.34679pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.67627pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{{k}{r}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.92825pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.92825pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.34679pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.67627pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{{k}{r}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.92825pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.92825pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.34679pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.67627pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{{k}{r}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.92825pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.92825pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.34679pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.67627pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\,\partial_{r}\mu_{\phi}(z)\,\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\mu_{\phi}(z)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{C^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}{k}}}}{C^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}{k}}}}{C^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}{k}}}}{C^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}{k}}}}

\displaystyle=0\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\text{with}\qquad\qquad\partial_{i}=\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}}

(22)

and the inverse metric
F^{ij}
. With the vanishing ACT the decoder geometry is Riemannian. For the rest of the paper we omit the network index
\phi
whenever possible.

Fisher Frobenius norm and ellipses

The classifier and decoder each construct a latent coordinate system
z
and a Fisher information or metric tensor. A straightforward measure for the magnitude of
F_{ij}
in the Euclidean sense is the Frobenius norm

\displaystyle\left\|F_{ij}\right\|=\sqrt{\sum_{ij}F_{ij}F_{ij}}\;.

(23)

It is not coordinate-invariant, but a direction-averaged summary of how stretched or
compressed distances are in the Fisher information metric compared to a Euclidean metric in the coordinate chart selected by the network during training. Larger Fisher distances imply that two instances or likelihoods are easier to distinguish in latent space.

Figure 4
:
Upper: test data and Frobenius norm of the Fisher information for a binary classifier (left) and a three-label classification (right). Lower: test data and Frobenius norm of the Fisher information for four labels (left) and Fisher ellipses for the three-label case (right).

In Fig.
4
we first see that the Frobenius norm traces decision boundaries in the latent classifier space. Because the Fisher information is the curvature of the log-likelihood surface, a large Frobenius norm signals high sensitivity of the classifier output to changes in
z
. At a decision boundary the predicted label changes most rapidly. In contrast, the Frobenius norm is small within the classes, where the classifier output hardly changes. While the Frobenius norm measures the discriminative power over the latent space, the encoded local information is averaged over all directions.

We resolve the directional stretch or compression through Fisher ellipses in the lower right panel of Fig.
4
. They are aligned with the eigenvectors of the Fisher information, and the inverse eigenvalue scales the corresponding axis. The eigenvalues tell us how much classification power a latent direction carries, so Fisher ellipses should align with decision boundaries. For our graphic representation, the ellipse areas are encoded in a color map, while their shown area is normalized. The information loss from the directional averaging of the Frobenius norm is indeed small for elongated ellipses close to the decision boundaries.

Fisher directional derivative

The eigenvector corresponding to the largest Fisher information eigenvalue marks the direction in which the network output is most sensitive to a latent space perturbation. For a classifier, we can test how the output features change along this direction. Given the leading Fisher information eigenvector
\upsilon
of the classifier, the directional derivative of the decoder, learning
\mu(z)
,

\displaystyle D_{\upsilon}\mu(z)=\partial_{\lambda}\mu(z+\lambda\upsilon)\Bigg\rvert_{\lambda=0}

(24)

locally quantifies how much the output features change along the most decisive direction.

Figure 5
:
Left: test data and Frobenius norm for the simplified classification of 1 vs. 7. Right: feature based on the size of the directional derivative of the decoder in the direction of the dominant eigenvector of the classifier metric.

The left panel of Fig.
5
shows the Frobenius norm and test data over the latent space. Here, and for all upcoming plots, we whiten the out of distribution region of the latent space, which results in a circular plot. For this example, we do not use the original MNIST data, but a toy version described in App.
B
. It consists of the digits 1 and 7 and each number is characterized by two features, the length of the top line
L_{\text{top}}
and the rotation angle of the vertical line
\theta
. The two numbers are separated with minimal overlap. In the right panel we show the Fisher directional derivative. For two features, it is also two-dimensional, and we color-code the feature with the larger component. The actual size of the directional derivative is encoded as increased transparency for smaller values. Because the features are standardized, the magnitudes of the derivatives are comparable.

First, we see that the directional derivative is largest around the decision boundary,
i.e.
the features change most rapidly there. Along the upper part of the decision boundary, the rotation angle of the vertical line is the dominant feature. This means that for numbers where the classifier is slightly uncertain, changing the angle is most effective to push it towards the other class. The upper examples of 1 and 7 illustrate this point. At the bottom of the latent space, the length of the vertical line has the larger impact on the classification. This reflects the geometry of this toy example, where 1 and 7 can be separated in different ways.

Away from the decision boundary the directional derivative for the two features has to be interpreted with care. For almost all of the 7-region, decreasing the top length moves the classifier output more towards a 1. This only implies that the top length is the feature that changes most when we move slightly towards the decision boundary. However, a very tilted 7 eventually needs to be rotated to become a 1 when we move classes.

Geometry alignment

Figure 6
:
Left: Classifier geometry with the Frobenius norm and ellipses. Center: Cosine similarity as alignment measure between the classifier and decoder geometry. Right: Decoder geometry with the Frobenius norm and ellipses.

To assess the quality of a set of features for a specific classification task, we measure the alignment between the two geometries with a cosine similarity between the two metric tensors,

\displaystyle\text{align}(z)=\frac{\sum_{i,j}\,{}^{\text{dec}}F_{ij}(z)\;{}^{\text{clf}}F_{ij}(z)}{\left\|{}^{\text{dec}}F_{ij}\right\|\;\left\|{}^{\text{clf}}F_{ij}\right\|}\;.

(25)

This is not an invariant scalar and only tests whether the orientation entailed in the two metric tensors is the same. The induced geometry from the decoder becomes interpretable once the reconstructed features are well understood. In the 1 vs. 7 example, the features represent physical characteristics of the digits, while before, when the full image is reconstructed, interpreting how the value of each pixel influences the space is difficult. The decoder geometry is much simpler, as it is built on a Gaussian distribution, which does not permit any skewness,
C_{ijk}=0
.

In Fig.
6
we show the Frobenius norms for the classifier and the decoder and the cosine similarity for the two spaces for the 1 vs. 7 binary classification. The near-perfect alignment close to the decision boundary implies that the the decoder is sensitive in the same direction as the classifier, so the chosen features are a good basis for the classification. It fails far away from the decision boundary, where the certain classifier is insensitive to small changes in the latent space, and the decoder still reconstructs the features. If the two spaces are aligned at the decision boundary, the chosen features are sufficient and do not carry unnecessary information for the classification.

Geodesics, autoparallels, and latent distance

Figure 7
:
Simplified 1 vs. 7 classification task. Left: three LC-geodesics with their Fisher-Rao distance
\rho_{F}
and the Euclidean distance
\rho_{E}
superimposed on the Frobenius norm. Right: reconstructed rotation angle and top length as a function of the Fisher-Rao distance along the geodesics.

We illustrate Fisher-Rao distances for the 1 vs 7 classification in Fig.
7
, where we show three LC-geodesics. The upper geodesic is limited to just the boundary region. All three clearly differ from Euclidean straight lines in
\mdmathbb{R}^{2}
, as does the statistical distance from the Euclidean distance
\rho_{E}
. Whenever we traverse regions of high Frobenius norm, the Fisher-Rao distance increases much faster than the Euclidean distance.

The right panels in Fig.
7
show the reconstructed features, rotation angle and top length, along each classifier geodesic. For the first geodesic, the features change approximately linearly with
\rho_{F}
from the classifier. If we instead measure the features along the decoder geodesics, they grow linearly by definition. Linear change of a feature along a classifier geodesic thus suggests an agreement between the classifier and decoder metric around the decision boundary, which confirms that the given feature is precisely what pushes events across the boundary. We confirm this by comparing with Fig.
5
, including the fact that the most relevant feature for the boundary crossing is the angle in this region.

For the second geodesic, the Frobenius norm in the black region is small and the Fisher-Rao distance grows more slowly than
\rho_{E}
at first. Conversely, the top length feature changes rapidly, indicating that the decoder and classifier metric define vastly different geometries. Closer to the decision boundary we find a region of linear growth of the rotation angle with the classifier distance
\rho_{F}
. Like for the first geodesic, the angle is the leading classification feature. Fig.
5
confirms this the top length is the dominant feature as one moves towards the decision boundary and is then surpassed by the rotation angle, which ultimately changes the classification. Beyond the boundary, the top length dominates again.

The third geodesic never crosses the decision boundary,
i.e.
the classification never changes and only becomes less certain. Since we never cross regions of high Frobenius norm, the flat Euclidean distance is significantly larger than the Fisher-Rao distance. The reconstruction in Fig.
7
shows an initially rapid change in top length, which is subsequently replaced by a period of more linear growth. Hence, the certainty in the classification drops at a rate similar to that with which the top length changes. This implies that the most influential feature for classification in this latent region is the top length, which is confirmed by Fig.
5
.

Interpretable coordinate systems

Because the likelihoods of our classifier form an exponential family
[
4
,
64
,
65
]
, their geometry is dually flat, meaning the Ricci scalars of both
(\pm 1)
-connections vanish everywhere. For that reason, there exist two privileged coordinate frames in which the
(\pm 1)
-autoparallels appear as straight lines
[
64
,
4
]
. These so-called natural and expectation coordinates have a straightforward statistical interpretation in that they correspond to log-likelihood ratios or likelihoods
[
64
]
.

For discrete likelihoods
p(k|z)
over classes
k
, an exponential family
\mathcal{P}_{e}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{e}=\left\{p_{z}\,|\,p(k|z)=h(k)\cdot\exp{\left(z^{\top}T(k)-A(z)\right)}\right\}\;,

(26)

with
h(k)
a function of the class
k
,
T(k)
a sufficient statistic of the class counts, and
A(z)
ensuring normalization
[
4
]
. The corresponding natural coordinate system
\lambda\in\mdmathbb{R}^{K-1}
is

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}=\log\frac{p(k|z)}{p(k_{0}|z)}\qquad\text{for}\qquad k_{0}\in K,\;k_{0}\neq k\;.

(27)

In these coordinates, the categorical likelihoods from Eq.(
19
) become

\displaystyle p_{\phi}(k|z)\;\mapsto\;p_{\phi}(k|\lambda)=\frac{\exp\sum_{i=1}^{K-1}(\lambda_{i}T_{i}(k))}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}\exp\sum_{i=1}^{K-1}\lambda_{i}(T_{i}(l))}\;,

(28)

with
T(k)
a one-hot encoding.

1.

The
(+1)
-autoparallels appear as straight lines in
\lambda
by definition, they are log linear interpolations between two distributions
p^{(0)}
and
p^{(1)}
at
\lambda^{(0,1)}
,

\displaystyle\lambda^{(t)}

\displaystyle=(1-t)\lambda^{(0)}+t\lambda^{(1)}\qquad\qquad t\in[0,1]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\qquad\log p^{(t)}(k)

\displaystyle=(1-t)\log p^{(0)}(k)+t\log p^{(1)}(k)-\log Z(t)

\displaystyle\qquad\text{with}\qquad Z(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\left[p^{(0)}(i)\right]^{1-t}\;\left[p^{(1)}(i)\right]^{t}\;.

(29)

2.

Conversely,
(-1)
-autoparallels are straight lines in the expectation coordinates

\displaystyle\eta_{i}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\langle T_{i}(k)\Bigr\rangle_{k\sim p(k|z)}\;,

(30)

and for the one-hot sufficient statistic we obtain
\eta_{i}=p(i|z)
. They are a linear mixture of the start and end distribution,

\displaystyle\eta^{(t)}

\displaystyle=(1-t)\eta^{(0)}+t\eta^{(1)}\qquad\qquad t\in[0,1]

\displaystyle p^{(t)}

\displaystyle=(1-t)p^{(0)}+tp^{(1)}\,.

(31)

Depending on which learned autoparallel is straight in these coordinate frames, we can infer whether the model exhibits a log-likelihood ratio interpolation or a linear (mixture) interpolation.

Figure 8
:
Left: Frobenius Norm and Chebyshev field with color coded norm for the three digit dataset. Three autoparallels connecting three test digits for the two
\alpha
connections. Right: Paths on the simplex along the autoparallels on the left.

In Fig.
8
we show the Chebyshev field over the latent space for the three digit classification from the right panels of Fig.
4
. We also show
(\pm 1)
-autoparallels connecting test points in the three class regions. The right panel shows the output of the classifier along these autoparallels. We observe a preference for natural coordinates from the almost straight latent curves from the
(+1)
-connection. In contrast, the
(-1)
connection enforces a curved latent path, but straight lines on the simplex through the linear mixture of probabilities, Eq. (
31
). This indicates that the classifier metric prefers the natural coordinates over the expectation coordinates.

The natural coordinates, representing the log likelihood ratio, are curved on the simplex, but the deviation from a straight line in
z
between the digits 0 and 2 and 2 and 1, respectively, is minor. The
(-1)
-autoparallels have to be straight lines on the simplex, so if we want to use them to connect two points of high certainty, we cannot go through the region of the third class. However, in our case, the direct path in latent space from 0 to 1 involves a section of non-zero probability for 2. This pattern is also visible in the direction and norm of the Chebyshev field, which can be viewed as a force pushing the
(+1)
-autoparallel away from the
(-1)
-autoparallel. This force is strongest at the decision boundary between 0 and 1, affected by a metric that is larger where the decision is clearer. Explicitly, we can infer something about how the digits are connected. Turning a 0 into 1 prefers a phase of non-zero probability for a 2. However, the shift from 0 to 2 and from 1 to 2, does not require such an intermediate step.

Novel nonmetricity scalars for information geometry

In general, statistical manifolds have both curvature and nonmetricity but, as shown in Eq.(
34
), this is not to the case for our flat but non-metric dual classifier geometry. This motivates us to propose novel informative scalars for information geometry. These are different from the magnitude of nonmetricity discussed in Ref.
[
38
]
, which refers to the constant Amari-
\alpha
scaling in Eq.(
7
).

The ACT is the nonmetricity tensor of the
(+1)
-geometry,
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{(+1)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-18.22963pt{(+1)}\kern 8.04631pt\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-42.56622pt\mathchoice{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.04631pt{i}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(+1)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-18.22963pt{(+1)}\kern 8.04631pt\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-42.56622pt\mathchoice{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.04631pt{i}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(+1)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-12.75465pt{(+1)}\kern 5.44908pt\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-30.13797pt\mathchoice{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.44908pt{i}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(+1)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-11.58798pt{(+1)}\kern 4.28241pt\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-28.9713pt\mathchoice{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[18.19606pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0722pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.19446pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.28241pt{i}}}}F_{jk}=C_{ijk}

[
20
,
4
]
, where a skewness in the likelihood implies more complex distributions than Gaussians. Geometrically, the nonmetricity tensor quantifies the non-preservation of lengths and angles on a manifold
[
60
]
. A finite ACT thereby signals a deviation from Riemannian geometry towards higher geometric complexity. Similarly to nonmetricity scalars in gravity theories, we propose different ACT contractions at quadratic order to access this information,

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle=C_{ijk}C^{ijk}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad C_{2}

\displaystyle=\tau_{i}\tau^{i}

\displaystyle C_{3}

\displaystyle=\tilde{C}_{ijk}\tilde{C}^{ijk}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad C_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{ijk}C^{ijk}-\tau_{i}\tau^{i}}{4}\;.

(32)

1.

C_{1}
is the complete contraction of the fully symmetric ACT. In affine differential geometry, its analogue is the Fubini-Pick invariant, where the Fubini-Pick form corresponds to our ACT
[
1
]
.

2.

C_{2}
is the contraction of the ACT trace or Chebyshev field.

3.

C_{3}
encodes the (non-symmetric) traceless portion of the ACT. Vanishing
C_{3}
indicates an additional conformal symmetry of the dual geometry. To see that, we look at the Chebyshev field
\tau^{i}
. In gravity, the corresponding Weyl field is eliminated locally by a conformal transformation,
g\mapsto e^{-\Phi/n}\cdot g
. This holds for the statistical manifold as well, because the Chebyshev field can be written as the gradient of a scalar potential,
\tau_{i}=\partial_{i}\Phi
. The potential
\Phi
then defines the proper conformal factor
e^{-\Phi/n}

[
60
]
. As the dual curvature scalars vanish for the classifier, they are unchanged by this transformation. The LC-curvature scalar changes under conformal transformations, with details discussed in App.
A
.

This implies that if
C_{3}=0
locally, a conformal transformation locally eliminates the skewness in the likelihoods and the lowest-order irremovable deviation from Gaussianity appears at fourth order. Conversely,
C_{3}\neq 0
is a local statement about the model complexity, indicating an irremovable asymmetry.

Figure 9
:
Binary classification: Left: test data and the scalar map
C_{1}
. Right: flow lines of the Chebyshev field
\tau
superimposed on
C_{1}
.

4.

C_{4}
is motivated by gravity theories, where a unique nonmetricity scalar
Q
ensures the equivalence of predictions from alternative formulations to those of General Relativity
[
60
,
39
]
,

\displaystyle Q=-\frac{1}{4}Q_{ijk}Q^{ijk}+\frac{1}{2}Q_{ijk}Q^{kji}+\frac{1}{4}\mathchoice{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\m
